5. developproperties.cpp, applyKeyToParams() -- dock key -> EditParams field, applying the DIVISOR scaling (a 0..100 slider backing a 0..1 field writes f / 100.0f).

6. developproperties.cpp, populateSlidersFromStack() -- the setSliderReal() call, the exact inverse of 5 (p.field * 100.0). If 5 and 6 disagree the value drifts a little further every time the user navigates away and back.

7-9. Presets -- four functions, and NOTE THE KEY NAMESPACE CHANGE:

- kBasicLeaves / kCurvesLeaves / kColorLeaves / kCalibrateLeaves / kColorGradeLeaves / kDetailLeaves / kEffectsLeaves in developproperties.cpp -- the checklist row's key + label. Params the panel treats as one control fold into ONE leaf (Grain = amount + size + roughness).

- leafChanged() -- is this leaf non-default? Tests the PRIMARY param(s) only, matching isIdentity().

- collectScopeLeaves() -- writes the values under their EditStack JSON FIELD names (raw, unscaled), not the dock slider keys.

- DevelopPresets::assignParam() in Develop/Presets/developpresets.cpp -- the other half: JSON field name -> field, raw, NO divisor scaling. applyKeyToParams (dock keys, scaled) and assignParam (JSON names, raw) are the two key namespaces, and the easiest thing in Develop to get wrong.

10. Multi-image editing -- kFloatFields (or kIntFields) in the anonymous namespace above DevelopProperties::diffParamFields. ONE row: the EditParams field name and a pointer to the member. The diff and the copy both walk that table, so a field missing from it is a field that silently does NOT propagate to the rest of the selection while every other slider does -- the worst kind of bug to notice. A NON-SCALAR field (the tone curve's arrays) cannot use a member pointer: give it a named pseudo-field plus a differs/copy pair beside the table, and wire both into diffParamFields AND copyParamFields -- see curvesDiffer / copyCurves.

11. History -- FREE for a normal slider: the generic tree-edit handler calls noteEdit with the caption and value, and noteEdit snapshots the whole EditStack. NOT free for a custom editor (the colour wheel, the WB row, the tone-range slider) -- those need their own noteEdit(action, value, mergeKey) call, and the mergeKey is what collapses a drag into one history row instead of forty.

12. This file -- for a slider like Grain that is four places: the pipeline order list under Architecture, the spatial-op description under Scope & mask model, the slider inventory under Develop dock (UI), and the leaf-folding note under Develop presets. Then sync the Table of Contents.

13. Docs/develophelp.html -- the in-app user help. Currently LAGS the panel (no Vignette or Grain entry), so this step is a known gap rather than an established habit.

CHANGING AN EXISTING OP'S MATH is a different job from adding a slider, and a riskier one: the ops shape recipes users have already saved, so a "harmless" rewrite silently re-renders their pictures. tests/unit/tst_localcontrast.cpp is the worked example -- capture golden values from the CURRENT build FIRST, confirm they pass before touching anything, then refactor and require them still to pass. Mutation-check the gate too (a 0.1% change in any tuning constant should fail it); a characterization test whose goldens match its own inputs proves nothing.

A WHOLE NEW SECTION (rather than a slider in an existing one) adds a fixed set of bookkeeping on top of the thirteen, and every item is one that fails SILENTLY if missed. Working examples: the Curves panel (grep PV_Curves) and the Detail panel (grep PV_Detail), which also shows what MOVING an existing control between sections costs -- see "Group->keys mapping" above.

- DevelopProperties::PreviewGroup gains PV_<Name>, and hdrStar[] grows by one.

- paramsGroup() / groupLabel() / previewFlag() -- three switches, plus a new EditParams::Group and its resetGroup() case.

- EditScope gains show<Name>, effectiveScopeParams() folds it, EditStack::isIdentity tests it, and toJson/fromJson read + write its key.

- updateSectionHeaderCaptions()'s hdrs[] table (the " *" edited marker) -- and GROW hdrStar[] with it: it is indexed by POSITION in hdrs[], so a new row with a too-short array walks off the end.

- buildTree(): the add<Name>() call in pipeline order, the eye-button reset, and the expand-state save/restore pair.
- kSections gains the section's leaf table.
- sectionHelp()'s switch + a Docs/develop<name>help.html page + its winnow.qrc entry.

- buildTree()'s <name>EyeBtn nulling, addHeader()'s eye-assignment chain, the expand-state capture/restore pair, and persistSectionExpanded()'s name list.

MASKABLE OR GLOBAL-ONLY? A per-scope adjustment belongs to a Group and is edited on whichever scope is active. A Global-only one (denoiseLuma / denoiseChroma, the tone splits) belongs to NO Group, cannot be previewed or reset per section, and must be read from scopes[0] in buildPreset() and written to scopes[0] in mergePreset() whatever scope is active.


⏺ Develop / Image Edit (Parametric Adjustments) > Export (Getting Developed Images Out)

"X" in Develop mode exports the SELECTED images with each image's own develop recipe applied. Everything about writing an image out of Winnow lives in one place, and the only thing that varies between the three callers is where the pixels come from.

WHY ONE ENGINE. Winnow had two unrelated export loops, and neither could do the job:

- MW::saveAsFile / SaveAsDlg rendered through Pixmap::load -- the BROWSE decode -- so it applied no develop recipe, embedded no ICC profile and copied no EXIF. Three formats, one quality spin, no naming or resizing.

- EmbelExport::exportImages had the mature batch (progress, Esc abort, subfolder destination, suffix, overwrite policy, ExifTool tag/ICC/thumbnail copy) but reads each file FROM DISK, so it cannot see a develop edit either, and its quality is hardcoded at 100.

A third copy was the wrong end state, so Export/ owns the shared engine. SaveAsDlg has been retired onto it (File > Save Preview as now opens the same dialog with the plain pixel source and its develop-only rows hidden). EmbelExport is NOT migrated yet; when it is, Develop -> Embellish composition becomes possible for the first time, because Embel's source becomes the developed QImage instead of QImage(fPath).

THE PIECES

  Export/exportsettings.h    ExportSettings (destination, naming, format, sizing) plus
                             the curated writable-format table.
  Export/exportpresets.*     Named presets in QSettings; the DevelopPresets contract.
  Export/imageexporter.*     ImageExporter: the batch engine.
  Dialogs/exportdlg.*        The dialog (hand-built, no .ui).

Utilities/tokenfilename.*  The shared {TOKEN} filename parser.

PIXEL SOURCE. ImageExporter owns destination resolution, naming, uniquify/skip/ overwrite, resizing, saving, colour-space tagging, the ExifTool copy, progress and abort. It does NOT generate pixels: a PixelSource callback does, and it is ASYNCHRONOUS BY CONTRACT --

  using Done = std::function<void(bool ok, const QImage &img)>;
  using PixelSource = std::function<void(const QString &fPath, Done done)>;

-- because one full-resolution raw render is ~1s and a synchronous batch would freeze the GUI for minutes. The batch is a CHAIN, not a loop: startNext() asks the source for one image and returns; onImageReady() saves it and queues the next through the event loop. That is what keeps progress painting and Cancel responsive throughout.

MW::developPixelSource is the develop provider and reuses renderDevelopFullResAsync's staging exactly: GUI thread captures the recipe and the metadata, developRenderPool decodes, GUI thread runs the mask prerequisites (ensureRangeRef / Subject / Sky / Depth / Object / BrushSam -- they are synchronous and keep path-keyed caches the live edit session also uses, so they must not race it), then developRenderPool composites. An image the exporter decoded ITSELF is dropped from WorkingImageCache afterwards, so a long batch does not evict the image the user is actively editing.

RAW DENOISE IS PART OF THE BASE, SO THE PROVIDER APPLIES IT ITSELF. "Denoise raw" (PMRID) is not a stage in developCompositeStack -- it is pre-demosaic, so it changes the WorkingImage the composite starts FROM. The interactive render reaches it through developRawDenoisedBase / ensureRawDenoise, and neither is usable here: both are keyed to the image on screen (developPmridFull / developDenoised), and a batch must not disturb the live edit session's caches. So developPixelSource re-decodes the mosaic with the denoiser on (ImageDecoder::decodeRawWorking) and blends toward the clean base by the stored amounts (Develop::BlendRawDenoise) -- the same two steps, without the caching. IT IS GATED ON THE RECIPE (EditParams::denoiseRaw via wantsDenoiseRaw), not on the amounts alone: EditParams defaults denoiseLuma to 0.75 and denoiseChroma to 1.0 -- NON-ZERO -- so "amounts > 0" is true for every raw including an unedited one, and testing only that would run PMRID on every image the exporter and the preview builder touch. The flag carries the stored intent and falls back to G::autoRunDenoise when the image says nothing, so this reads exactly the answer the interactive render reads. It is a global rather than an MW member because Metadata::defaultRenderKey has to hash it from decoder threads. It reuses developPmridFull when that base belongs to the image being rendered (keyed path+iso, amount-independent), so exporting the image you are editing does not re-run the model. Gated on the GUI thread (rawDenoiseAvailable reads session state); the decode goes to developRenderPool. Only the COMPOSITE gets the denoised base -- orientation and the mask prerequisites stay on the clean one, exactly as renderDevelopFullResAsync splits src from work, so a mask selects the same pixels either way. Before this the exporter composited the clean cached WorkingImage regardless, and an image the user had denoised exported clean -- and the devPreview builder, which shares this provider, wrote that clean render under a recipe key asserting it matched the loupe.

PER-IMAGE RECIPES. DevelopProperties::stackJobFor(fPath) is the export twin of stackJob(): it reads the image's OWN stored EditStack (stackFor loads the sidecar on first touch), ignores the History hover override (a hovered row must never reach a file) and ignores the Transform / Replace preview eyes (those are VIEW toggles -- an export always renders the stored recipe in full). Both share buildStackJob().

prepareExport() calls flushPropagation() + flushAll() FIRST. A develop edit across a multi-image selection is batched (kPropagateMs), and the exporter reads stored stacks, so without the flush an export fired straight after a slider drag would write the pre-edit recipe for every image except the current one.

FORMATS. jpg, png, tif, heic and bmp, resolved at runtime against QImageWriter::supportedImageFormats() so a missing plugin DROPS the entry rather than failing at save time -- HEIC writes on macOS via the bundled qmacheif plugin and is simply absent on a stock Windows build. The table says which controls each format supports (depth, quality, TIFF compression) and the dialog HIDES the rest -- caption and control both, in ExportDlg::updateEnabledStates. A setting the format does not have is not one that could be switched on, so greying it would only invite the user to look for the switch. (Greying is kept for controls whose row IS live but currently irrelevant: the destination folder field, the sizing spin boxes.) Nothing in the dialog hard-codes a format name.

Every hideable control shares a row with one that never hides -- Depth and Quality with Type, Compression with Space -- so no row can empty out and leave a gap, and the group is the same height for every format. Both caption COLUMNS keep the width measured at build time, so nothing shifts sideways as the format changes either.

DESTINATION. Three choices, widest scope first in the dialog: Source folder (beside the originals), Source subfolder (a named subfolder of each image's own folder, created on demand) and Folder (one chosen folder for the whole batch). ExportSettings::Dest numbers them 0 SubfolderOfSource, 1 ChosenFolder, 2 SourceFolder -- SourceFolder is APPENDED rather than inserted in reading order because "dest" is a published preset key and renumbering would silently reinterpret every stored preset.

OVERWRITING THE ORIGINALS. Source folder + no suffix + no template + a format whose extension matches the source resolves to the source path itself. Only exists == Overwrite actually destroys anything (Unique name and Skip already handle it), so ExportDlg::onExportClicked counts those collisions and requires a confirmation naming the count, defaulting to Cancel. Reachable before via Folder pointed at the source folder; the Source folder option just makes it one click away.

AFTER THE BATCH (MW::onExportFinished). Nothing on screen changes unless the export landed in a folder the datamodel has loaded. When it did, "Insert exported images without rescanning the folder" picks HOW they appear: ticked inserts the written files into the open model in place (insertFiles), unticked rescans the folders (refresh). Both end up showing the new files -- the checkbox is insert-vs-rescan, not show-vs-hide, which is what its old label ("Add exported images to the folder view") got wrong. The setting key stays addToFolderView.

The checkbox is DISABLED unless a destination folder is loaded, since it can have no effect otherwise (ImageExporter::willTouchLoadedFolder, re-evaluated on every edit; for a subfolder-of-source destination it tests one folder per distinct SOURCE folder, not one per file). Its checked state is left alone while disabled, so pointing the export back at an open folder restores the user's choice.

DataModel::isFolderLoaded is the single definition of "is this folder loaded" -- the dialog's enable test and onExportFinished's act-on test must not disagree about a folder spelled with a trailing slash. folderSet answers the common case in O(1), with a QDir::cleanPath comparison as the fallback.

16-BIT. OutputTransform::ToImage16 is the 16-bit twin of ToImage: same tone curve and transfer function, packed to Format_RGBX64. The develop pipeline is float throughout, so the 8-bit pack was the only place precision was lost. The depth is threaded through WorkingImageCache::render / renderStack (OutDepth, defaulting to Eight so every interactive caller is untouched) and developComposite / developCompositeStack.

Three later stages had to be taught 16 bits or they would have silently flattened the whole frame:

- Straighten (CropTransform::applyGeometry) converted to ARGB32 for the transparent corner wedges; it now picks RGBA64 for a deep image.

- Warp (rectifyPerspective) round-tripped through 8-bit BGRA OpenCV; it now warps CV_16UC4 when the source is deep, with the opacity cut-off scaled to match.

- Spot heals are 8-bit by nature (LaMa / MI-GAN are 8-bit models, and the clone path works in ARGB32). applySpots() heals an 8-bit COPY and writes back only the pixels that actually CHANGED, promoted 257x. Healed pixels are synthesized so 8-bit precision there costs nothing, and every untouched pixel keeps its 16 bits.

COLOUR SPACE. sRGB, Display P3 or Adobe RGB, chosen in the dialog and carried in the preset (OutputSpace). Every export is TAGGED -- exports used to be untagged, which left every viewer guessing.

ImageExporter::renderSpace() maps the export layer's published OutputSpace onto OutputTransform::Space; the two enums stay separate because Develop must not depend on Export. The two pixel sources then reach the same place from opposite directions:

Develop  developPixelSource passes the space down through developCompositeStack -> WorkingImageCache::renderStack -> OutputTransform, which ENCODES in it, and tags the finished image with OutputTransform::ColorSpaceOf. save() sees a tag that already matches and writes it through untouched. The tag is set at the export boundary, NOT inside the composite: the spot and geometry stages round-trip through OpenCV and fresh QImages, which drop it. Preview  File > Save Preview as hands over the plain browse decode, so save() CONVERTS it to the target with Qt (an untagged decode is taken as sRGB, which is what untagged means in practice). Verified that Qt's conversion works in place for RGB888, RGBX64, ARGB32 and RGB32.

THE ICC TRAP: copySourceMetadata used to copy the source's ICC profile onto the export. That was always questionable -- a raw's profile describes the CAMERA space and a JPEG's describes what it was shot in, neither of which survives the develop pipeline -- and with a selectable output space it would flatly mislabel the file. The copy now excludes it (ExifTool::copyAllTags gained excludeIcc, which adds --icc_profile:all, rather than trusting -all:all's block-tag behaviour); the profile the writer embedded is the one that describes the pixels.

Wide-gamut output at 8 bits can band in smooth gradients -- the dialog's tooltip says so and points at 16-bit.

FIXED SIZE. The dialog is not resizable: ExportDlg::fitToContents pins it to the design width (kDialogWidth, 820) and to whatever height the layout asks for. The fields are sized to their content rather than to their grid column -- template 290, suffix 165, Type and Space 130 (they hold "Display P3"), Depth and Compression 100 -- so a wider window would show nothing more, and a taller one would just pool space under the last section. fitToContents is re-run whenever the form's height can change -- which is setBusy adding or removing the progress bar, and nothing else: changing FORMAT never changes the height, because every control a format hides shares a row with one it cannot hide.

QUALITY SITS ON THE TYPE ROW, in the same cells as Depth, because no format has both: quality belongs to the lossy formats (JPEG, HEIC) and depth to the lossless ones (PNG, TIFF). Only one is ever visible, so the group is two rows for every format instead of three with a hole in it, and the slider gets the width Depth's 100px combo would have wasted (it runs out through the slack column, with its value at the right edge). A format with BOTH -- a 16-bit lossy format -- would draw them on top of each other and needs its own row back.

The Name and Format grids are two layouts in two group boxes, so buildUi lines them up by hand: same margins, same column gap, both caption columns pinned to the widest caption in EITHER grid, each field column pinned to its own field, and the captions RIGHT-aligned so each sits hard against the control it names.

WHICH COLUMN TAKES THE SLACK is the part that is easy to get wrong, and it must not be one of the four. Stretch on the FIELD columns does not work however equal, because the fields are fixed-width: a stretchy field column cannot use the space it is given, and QGridLayout passes the leftover to whatever can grow, i.e. the caption columns. The two grids have different field widths (290 + 165 against 130 + 100), so the leftovers differed too and their caption columns came out different widths -- which is what put Type and Space right of Template even with both caption columns pinned to the same minimum. Stretching the middle caption column instead fails the other way: it pushes the second pair out to the right edge and opens a chasm between the pairs.

So the slack sits in a FIFTH, empty column past the last field. Columns 0-3 then hold their pinned widths: column 0 is shared, so Type and Space start on the same x as Template, and each second pair follows its OWN first field (Suffix after the 290px template combo, Depth and Compression after the 130px type combo plus a 50px gap, kFormatPairGap, added to that grid's caption column). The two rows meant to use the whole width -- the exists radios, and the example + Template editor button -- span into the slack column to keep it. The Format group's two checkboxes are in the grid too, on the field column, so they line up with the combos above them; they go in AFTER the row-height pass so they keep their natural height rather than a field's. The Size group has no captions of its own and borrows the same geometry: a column 0 of the caption width, so its radios and Don't enlarge start on that one left edge too. That column is held open by a stand-in WIDGET, not by a column minimum alone -- QGridLayout only puts its column spacing between columns that hold something, so a merely-wide column 0 starts column 1 a whole gap left of where the other groups start theirs.

Two measurement rules make that arithmetic safe. The captions are ensurePolished() BEFORE being measured -- a size hint is not final until the style and the stylesheet have been applied, which otherwise happens on the first layout pass, leaving the measurement short. And every caption is then FIXED at the measured width rather than the column merely being floored at it, so column 0 is exactly capW0 in every grid and a group that lines up by arithmetic cannot drift from one that lines up by being in the grid. A fixed-width label no longer fills its cell, though, and an item that cannot fill its cell sits at the LEFT of it -- so the caption ITEMS are aligned right as well, or Depth and Compression drift to the far side of their kFormatPairGap- widened column instead of staying against their combos.

WATCH THE GLOBAL STYLESHEET when fixing a control's width. G::css sets "min-width: 6em" on every QComboBox and "min-width: 100px" on every QPushButton (Main/widgetcss.cpp), and Qt applies a stylesheet min-width by writing it onto the widget's minimumWidth when the widget is POLISHED. That write ALWAYS LANDS LAST -- after setFixedWidth in buildUi, and after an ensurePolished() meant to get ahead of it (tried; it does not work) -- so an unguarded control silently takes the global width: ~130px for a combo, 112 for a button (100 content + 5+5 padding + 1+1 border, since a stylesheet width is the CONTENT box).

A per-widget sheet is the only thing the re-polish cannot overrule, because it is what the re-polish applies. Two shapes of that, both in use here:

- Say the width IN the sheet, min-width AND max-width, converted to content units (sizeButton, minus kButtonChrome). Works anywhere; the cost is that kButtonChrome has to agree with widgetcss.cpp's QPushButton padding and border.

- Clear the floor with "min-width: 0px" and let a pinned GRID COLUMN supply the width, the control being Expanding with setFixedWidth as its cap (the Format combos). A maximum is safe -- the global rules set no max-width. Needs a column to pin, which the preset buttons do not have.

Clearing the floor WITHOUT something to hold the control open leaves it at its own text width: that is how the preset buttons came out New 41px, Update 55, Rename 65, Delete 52, all asked for 75.

THE PRESET BAR IS ONE ROW: the combo and New / Update / Rename / Delete together, with the combo Expanding so the preset NAME takes whatever the four buttons and the "modified" label leave. The buttons were moved off their own second row (and cut from 100px to kPresetBtnWidth) to buy that line back.

FORMATTED LIKE INGESTDLG: QGroupBox sections whose title is two points up on the body text, and 32px-high buttons. ExportDlg::addSection makes the box, so every section (Preset included) is dressed the same way and the callers only see the layout they add their rows to. An earlier compact formatting -- bold caption over a rule, a tinted bordered panel for the presets -- was carried alongside this one behind a scratch flag, G::useExportDlgIngestStyle, to compare the two in one build; the Ingest style won and the flag and the other branch were deleted 2026-08-07.

Note the group-box title font is set on the BOX, and a widget's font propagates to its children, so addSection puts the controls in an inner widget whose font is reset to the dialog's -- otherwise every control inherits the enlarged title size.

THE PRESET BAR keeps its selection while you edit. Changing a setting marks the preset "modified" (the label beside the combo) and ENABLES Update, which writes the form back to the still-selected preset; Update is disabled while the form matches. The combo must NOT be reset to "(none)" on an edit: it is the only record of which preset Update targets, so clearing it makes Update unreachable in the one situation it exists for -- you can only want it after editing, and editing would have cleared it. Rename and Delete act on the STORED preset, so they stay enabled whether or not the form has been edited, and a Rename leaves an edited form still modified.

EXPORT PRESETS are COMPLETE, unlike develop presets. A develop preset is partial on purpose (an absent key means "don't touch that adjustment"); an export preset is a whole ExportSettings INCLUDING the destination, which is what lets Develop > Export with preset > <name> run with no dialog at all. An absent key falls back to the ExportSettings default, so an old preset survives a new field. The same versioning contract as develop presets applies -- see "Stale presets and history" below: add keys freely, never rename or rescale one in place.

THE TOKEN PARSER. {TOKEN} filename templates were parsed by two identical private copies (IngestDlg::parseTokenString and RenameFileDlg::parseTokenString). Adding a third for Export would have been three. The language now lives in Utilities/tokenfilename.h -- one ordered table driving the token list, the example map and the parser -- and Ingest and Rename delegate to it, so a token added there appears in all three at once.

THE TEMPLATE EDITOR is likewise the one editor (Dialogs/tokendlg.h). File naming's Template editor button, on the right of the Example row, opens TokenDlg on MW::filenameTemplates -- the same map Ingest and Rename edit, held by REFERENCE by the dialog and persisted by MW -- so a template created while exporting is offered by the other two immediately, and vice versa. That shared map is why ExportDlg takes it

  non-const.

Afterwards the combo is rebuilt and lands back on the template it was on: by key, then (a RENAME moves the key while the token string stays put) by token string, then the first template there is, which is where a DELETED one has to end up. The rebuild is signal-blocked -- it is not a user edit -- so the preset is only marked modified when the token string the export would actually use has changed.

THE COMBO LISTS EXACTLY THE TEMPLATES, in the editor's own order -- the two are views of one map and must not disagree. It offers no synthetic "no template" entry: Ingest and Rename do not either, and keeping the original name IS a template ("{ORIGINAL FILENAME}"). The entry ExportDlg used to prepend, "Original file name", sat one space away from the real "Original filename" template that does the same thing, so the combo listed four things where the editor listed three. Like the other two dialogs, ExportDlg seeds the map with the stock pair if it is empty.

ExportSettings::tokenTemplate is still allowed to be EMPTY -- it is a published preset key, older presets hold it, and the engine reads it as "keep the original base name". Loading one selects the template that keeps the original name, or the first one.


⏺ Develop / Image Edit (Parametric Adjustments) > Stale Presets and History (Schema Drift)

What happens to a user's saved presets and their History when an adjustment is later renamed, rescaled or removed. The two behave completely differently.

HISTORY IS IMMUNE. Entries are in-memory EditStack structs, session scoped (see Develop history above): they are discarded on quit and nothing is written to the sidecar. A snapshot therefore can never be older than the binary holding it, so no migration is possible or needed. The only drift is cosmetic -- an entry's action label is the slider caption FROZEN as a string at record time, so renaming a caption mid-session leaves earlier rows reading the old name until the next launch.

EXPORT PRESETS have the same exposure and the same contract (Export/exportpresets.h): they live in QSettings indefinitely and are applied by assigning stored values into an ExportSettings, so add keys freely and never rename or rescale one in place. They are less fragile in one respect -- read() starts from a default-constructed ExportSettings and overwrites field by field, so a key added after a preset was saved keeps its struct default instead of reading back as 0. When a key genuinely must change meaning: bump kVersion and convert in migrate(), which runs inside read() before any value reaches the struct.

PRESETS ARE THE EXPOSURE POINT. They live in QSettings indefinitely, key by key, and are applied by assigning the stored value straight into an EditParams field. Three ways a key goes stale, in increasing order of harm:

- REMOVED key -- harmless. read() returns it, mergePreset() hands it to assignParam(), an if / else-if chain with no final else, so an unknown key is silently ignored. The preset applies minus that one setting.

- RENAMED key -- silent data loss. Same mechanism as removed: the user's preset quietly stops carrying that adjustment, with no warning and nothing in the UI to show it happened.

- RESCALED or REDEFINED key -- silent CORRUPTION, the case that actually matters. assignParam writes the stored float straight into the field, so if a 0..1 field becomes 0..100 (or an EV becomes a percentage, or the op's maths is retuned) every old preset applies a wildly wrong value and reads to the user as broken.

THE RULE: treat a preset key as a published format. ADD keys freely -- an absent key is a no-op both in a preset (childKeys() reports only what was written) and in a sidecar (paramsFromJson defaults it). NEVER rename or rescale one in place.

THE MIGRATION HOOK exists for when that is genuinely unavoidable. Every preset carries a schema stamp: DevelopPresets::write() stores `version = kVersion` at the preset root (beside sourceScope, so it never lands in the Global/Edits key sweeps), and read() calls migrate() before returning -- i.e. before any value reaches assignParam. To change an existing key: bump kVersion and append ONE ordered step to migrate(), if (p.version < N) { ...rewrite the affected keys in p.params / p.globals...; } so an old preset falls through every intervening step in turn. A preset stamped NEWER than kVersion (a downgraded build) is passed through untouched -- assignParam already ignores keys it does not recognise, which degrades better than guessing backwards.

Presets written before the stamp existed have no version key and read back as 1, which is correct by definition -- v1 is the schema they were written against -- so the default in read() must stay 1 forever. They are NOT rewritten on startup: they get their stamp the next time that preset is saved or renamed, and until then behave identically. Sidecars have the same hook available via EditParams::version, which is persisted and round-tripped but not yet branched on.


⏺ Develop / Image Edit (Parametric Adjustments) > Corrupted or Changed Develop Settings

Develop state is read back from three stores that the app does not fully control: the per-image XMP sidecar (a user file that a crash, a sync client or a hand edit can damage), the preset store in QSettings, and the general settings.ini. Forward tolerance handles values that are ABSENT. This section is about values that are PRESENT but wrong -- unreadable, out of range, or naming something this build has no code for, like an unknown demosaic engine.

THE OLD FAILURE MODE was uniform and silent: every one of these read paths fell back to a default without telling anyone. A corrupt sidecar came back as "this image has no edits" -- indistinguishable from an untouched image -- and the user's next edit overwrote what was still on disk.

WHAT IS VALIDATED

- Sidecar (EditStack::fromBase64) now returns a Status -- Ok / Absent / Corrupt -- so callers can tell "unedited" from "unreadable", plus a list of repairs. A stack stamped newer than EditStack::kVersion is reported.

- EditStack::sanitize / sanitizeParams range-check every value that reaches the render pipeline, against the SAME limits the panel sliders enforce, so a repaired value is always one the user could have dialled in. It rejects NaN and infinity (one NaN turns the whole image black), orders the three tone splits, repairs a degenerate crop (a zero or negative width divides by zero downstream), and drops a warp whose quad did not survive (geometryFromJson only fills quad when the array is exactly 8 long, so a truncated one would otherwise warp through the DEFAULTS).

- Mask components whose tool or op is outside its enum are DROPPED, not rendered: both enums are append-only, so an out-of-range value is either corruption or a tool from a newer build, and neither has geometry this build can interpret.

- Presets are range-checked too, in mergePreset -- their values come from QSettings as bare QVariants and reach EditParams through a plain toFloat().
- The raw decode engine is validated in three places (see below).

THE DEMOSAIC ENGINE, the worst of the silent cases. G::DecodeRawEngine has two enumerators and three code paths tested against them, so a value that is NEITHER does not merely pick the wrong decoder -- it fails every test. RawFormat skips the Apple branch AND the winnow-only PMRID raw denoise, and the user gets un-denoised raws with no indication why. Three ways in, all now closed:

- settings.ini: MW::loadSettings cast the stored int straight to the enum. It is now range-checked before the cast.

- A preset: applyPreset tested `== "apple"` and treated everything else as winnow. It now matches both tokens explicitly and warns on anything else.

- Cross-platform: the Apple engine is macOS-only and its branch is compiled out elsewhere, but MW's demosaicEngineChanged handler set it unconditionally -- so a preset built on a Mac silently disabled raw denoise on Windows. Now clamped.

WHEN TO NOTIFY (and when not to)

Notify when the user would otherwise discover the loss themselves: settings that could not be read, a mask that will not be drawn, values that had to be limited, a store written by a newer Winnow. Do NOT notify for the cases forward tolerance exists to absorb -- an absent sidecar (the normal state of an unedited image) or an unknown KEY in an otherwise valid record, which is by design.

ANTI-SPAM IS THE HARD PART. Sidecars are read on EVERY image the user visits, so a damaged folder could fire one popup per file -- arrow-keying through fifty of them would bury the app. DevelopProperties::warnOnce therefore reports each KIND of problem at most once per session (warnedIssues holds the kinds already shown) while still logging every occurrence, and the message says other images may be affected. Startup settings use a second channel: MW::loadSettings runs long before G::newPopUp exists, so those messages queue in G::startupWarnings and MW::whenActivated shows them as ONE popup once the window is up.

A CORRUPT SIDECAR IS NEVER REWRITTEN BEHIND THE USER. The damaged file stays on disk -- it may be recoverable, and a silent overwrite is the exact failure this path exists to prevent. It is replaced only when the user edits that image, which is an explicit act, and the popup says so.

AND WINNOW NO LONGER CREATES ONE. Xmp::writeSidecar used to truncate the open handle with resize(0) and write the document in place, so a crash, a power loss or a full volume between the two left an empty or half-written sidecar -- and the sidecar is the only copy of the develop recipe, the rating, the label, the title and the copyright. It is now a QSaveFile: a temporary is written and renamed over the original, so what is on disk is either the whole old document or the whole new one. It also treats a SHORT write as a failure; only a return of zero was tested before, so a partial write on a full disk was reported to the caller as success.

Develop previews are why this stopped being theoretical. The 256px tier puts a base64 JPEG in the sidecar, roughly ten times the bytes the file used to carry, so the write window is ten times longer -- and topUpDevPreviews rewrites sidecars for images the user merely VIEWED, so the window is entered far more often than an edit session would suggest. The caller's QFile is closed inside writeSidecar, because on Windows the rename fails while another handle to the target is open and every caller opens the sidecar ReadWrite to construct the Xmp.


⏺ Develop / Image Edit (Parametric Adjustments) > Original and Developed Previews (origPreview / devPreview)

TWO PICTURES, TWO WORDS. Every image Winnow shows has an origPreview -- the camera's embedded JPEG, or the plain decode of a JPG/TIFF/PNG, the picture as shot -- and, once it has been developed, a devPreview: the render of its develop recipe. The original Winnow design predates the Develop module and had only one word, "preview", for both, and once developed thumbnails started appearing in the icon grid the two began to disagree in a way the user could see. The code now says which one it means: devPreview / DevPreviewCache / loadDevThumb for the developed picture, origPreview (G::WidthOrigPreviewColumn, ImageMetadata::widthOrigPreview) for the camera's. Not everything called "preview" is either of these -- previewActive is a History hover, previewShown is a section eye, maskRangePreview is a tool overlay, renderDevelopPreview is the live interactive render -- and those keep their names.

THE ON-DISK NAMES DO NOT CHANGE. The XMP attributes winnow:Develop, winnow:DevelopPreview and winnow:DevelopPreviewKey (Metadata/xmp.cpp) and the devPreview index's column names are a published format. Sidecars written by shipped builds must keep loading, so those strings keep their old spelling regardless of what the code around them is called. The two TableView header strings "WidthPreview" / "HeightPreview" stay too, for a smaller version of the same reason: column visibility is persisted in QSettings under the header string, so renaming them would silently reset the user's show/hide choice.

WHY ANY OF THIS EXISTS. Winnow keeps no catalogue -- only the images in the datamodel exist as far as the app is concerned. That left the develop recipe in each sidecar with no visual counterpart anywhere: the icon grid showed the camera's JPG, so a developed image looked exactly like an untouched one, and entering Develop on a raw spent 2-3 seconds displaying the UNDEVELOPED picture while the scene-linear decode ran. devPreviews close both gaps without building a library.


⏺ Develop / Image Edit (Parametric Adjustments) > Original and Developed Previews > Which Picture Is Shown

THE CONTROL IS A PICTURE CHOICE, NOT A MODE CONTROL. The status bar's leftmost dropdown (previewSourceCombo, MW::createStatusBar in initialize.cpp) reads Original / Developed, and its rows ARE the G::PreviewSource values, so no mapping table is needed. The same choice is on the View menu (Show Original / Show Developed, an exclusive pair) and on Y.

DEVELOP MODE IS DELIBERATELY NOT AN ITEM IN IT. It was, briefly, on the reasoning that Original and Developed are both Preview mode and so are "the alternative" to Develop. That is true of the plumbing and false of the control: Develop is an editing MODE, a different kind of thing from a choice of picture, and as a third peer item it read as though it were one of three pictures. It is also redundant -- D enters Develop and E / G / T leave it. So the combo answers exactly one question, and the mode is not surfaced in it at all.

THE CHOICE DOES NOT EXIST IN DEVELOP MODE, so it is disabled there rather than obeyed. Develop shows the developed image in the loupe -- a live render of the recipe being edited -- and the thumbnails must agree with it, so offering Original would let the user ask for a picture Develop cannot show. MW::syncPreviewSourceEnabled greys the combo (which then reads Developed) and disables the two menu items and Y with a reason; a disabled QAction greys its menu item AND stops its shortcut firing, so one gate covers the menu and the key, and the reason surfaces through the Disabled Shortcut Feedback path.

G::previewSource ITSELF IS NOT CHANGED BY THE MODE. The combo reads Developed in Develop as a statement about the mode, not as a new setting: entering Develop must not silently rewrite a Preview-mode preference the user gets back the moment they leave. syncPreviewSourceEnabled is called by both setOperationMode and setPreviewSource, so the control can never drift from the pair (mode, previewSource).

THE EFFECTIVE THUMBNAIL SOURCE IS THE PAIR, NOT THE SETTING:

    (G::operationMode == Develop) ? Developed : G::previewSource

which is exactly the test Thumb::loadDevThumb makes. So a MODE change moves it too, and MW::refreshDevelopThumbs -- the icon re-read described below -- runs on entering AND leaving Develop, not only on setPreviewSource. Without that, entering Develop with Original chosen leaves the camera thumbnails on screen while the loupe shows the developed image. setOperationMode skips the re-read when Developed is chosen, because then the effective source is Developed in both modes and there is nothing to redo -- which matters, since Developed is the default and this would otherwise re-read every developed thumbnail on every D press.

Y LEAVES DEVELOP RATHER THAN DOING NOTHING. In Develop mode "which picture" is not a question -- the loupe is showing a live render of the recipe being edited -- so MW::togglePreviewSource switches to Preview mode first. That is what makes Y usable as a before/after key while editing. Y is deliberately NOT in developShortcuts: the arbiter passes it through to the global QAction (see "Develop Mode Shortcut Arbiter").

DEVELOP MODE ALWAYS SHOWS DEVELOPED, whatever G::previewSource says -- you cannot edit what you cannot see. Both read paths test the mode first: Thumb::loadDevThumb and ImageDecoder::loadDevPreview.

CHANGING IT REBUILDS BOTH CACHES, and this is why it is a deliberate user action rather than something that fires on selection. MW::setPreviewSource is modelled on toggleUseRaw for exactly this reason: the setting changes what a CACHED DECODE MEANS, so a repaint is not enough.

- The icons (MW::refreshDevelopThumbs), because the developed thumbnail OVERWRITES the row's icon (DataModel::setDevelopIcon) -- the camera thumb is not kept alongside it, so getting it back means re-reading it. The rows that carry a devPreview key are cleared, both delegates' per-row pixmap caches are dropped, and reloadIconChunk re-dispatches MetaRead, which loads the visible rows first. Rows with no devPreview key are left alone: they have one picture, so re-reading them would cost a folder's worth of I/O to produce identical pixels.

  CLEARING AN ICON IS NOT ENOUGH TO GET IT RE-READ. MetaRead remembers every row a reader has returned for the current folder (readSuccessThisCycle) and needToRead short-circuits on it. It re-arms rows whose icon is missing in its JIT block -- but ONLY when the icon chunk is a WINDOW, because in the default brute-force mode (G::useJitIconCache false) it assumes a loaded icon stays loaded, and the loop would otherwise walk every row in the folder on every file selection. Nothing violated that assumption until previews started discarding icons. MW::setPreviewSource and MW::devPreviewUpdated therefore call MetaRead::invalidateLoadedIcons (queued, ahead of reloadIconChunk's own queued call), which makes the next setStartRow re-arm once. Symptom when this is missed: the thumbnails clear and never come back.
- The full-size image cache, because ImageDecoder consults the flag to decide whether to serve the devPreview or decode the file.

Only images that HAVE a devPreview change appearance; the rest re-read to the same pixels. That is wasted work on an unedited folder, and it is the price of not keeping two icons per row in memory for a folder of thousands.

THE AMBER "HAS EDITS" BADGE IS DRIVEN BY THE RECIPE (G::DevelopColumn), never by whether a preview exists, so an edited image whose preview could not be rendered still reads as edited -- and so does one being shown as Original.


⏺ Develop / Image Edit (Parametric Adjustments) > Original and Developed Previews > The Two Tiers

TIER 1, THE THUMBNAIL (256px, matching G::maxIconSize), lives IN THE XMP SIDECAR as winnow:DevelopPreview, base64, roughly 15-20 KB. It is in the sidecar so it travels with the image: a move, a rename, a copy to the other machine all carry it for free, and Winnow already trashes the sidecar with the file. It needs no cache, no index and no orphan handling of any kind.

TIER 2, THE devPreview, lives in AppDataLocation/PreviewCache as <id>.jpg, indexed by the devpreview table in index.db beside it (Cache/devpreviewcache.h, Cache/cachedb.h). It is out of band because several MB per image in every sidecar is not a trade worth making, and because losing it costs only a re-render.

FULL SENSOR RESOLUTION IS THE DEFAULT, and it is what makes the devPreview more than a placeholder. At full size the loupe can show AND ZOOM the developed picture with no raw decode at all. The cost is real -- an entry is several MB rather than a few hundred KB -- so it is a preference (G::devPreviewMaxEdge: Full / 4096 / 2560, "Developed preview size") sitting next to a cache limit that defaults to 20 GB rather than 2. The LRU byte cap is now load-bearing rather than theoretical.

SIZE AND QUALITY ARE SEPARATE PREFERENCES, because they buy different things. "Developed preview quality" (G::devPreviewQuality) is the JPEG quality the devPreview tier is written at, TYPED AS A NUMBER from 40 to 100, default 90. A number rather than a few named steps because there is no quality a preview is too poor to be worth offering: this is a cache of pictures to look at, not masters, and how far it can be pushed depends on the images and the eye -- q50 exports look fine on plenty of material. The floor of 40 is where JPEG starts showing blocking on smooth tone at any zoom, and a stored value outside 40-100 is treated as damaged and ignored (Main/settings.cpp), not clamped.

ONE NON-LINEARITY IS WORTH KNOWING at the top of the range: Qt's JPEG handler disables chroma subsampling only ABOVE 90 (qjpeghandler.cpp), so 91 writes 4:4:4 and roughly doubles the file over 90, which is the best quality that still subsamples 4:2:0. The jump in size between 90 and 91 is far larger than the jump in appearance, and nothing between 91 and 100 costs much more than 91 does.

THE THUMBNAIL TIER IS NOT COVERED BY IT and stays at a fixed 85. It is a 256 px grid icon of about 15-20 KB living in the sidecar; the preference exists to govern what the loupe shows at 100%, and turning it down would degrade the grid to save nothing worth saving. Measured on a 61MP source, dropping the thumbnail from 85 to 50 saves 13 KB per image (25.4 KB -> 12.4 KB, or 34 KB -> 17 KB once base64'd) -- which is 0.12% of what that image's full-res devPreview costs, and it comes out of SIDECARS, not out of the cache the byte cap governs. The quality side is asymmetric and goes the other way: a full-res preview is almost always viewed DOWNSCALED (61MP into a screen is a 4-8x reduction, which averages JPEG artifacts away), which is exactly why q50 is sound there, while a 256px thumbnail is viewed at ~1:1 in the grid, where an 8x8 JPEG block is ~3% of the icon's width and nothing hides it.

THE PROXY MAY NOT SERVE THIS TIER, only the full-res frame (or a proxy that already meets a finite cap). MW's devPreview provider hands back whichever faithful frame it has for the recipe in force, and the interactive proxy is sized to the VIEW (~1600 px), so an image flushed before its settle render landed was cached at proxy size and the loupe then showed a visibly soft picture where the preference said "Full size". Worse, it looked current forever: staleness is keyed on the RECIPE HASH, which says nothing about resolution. It was the common case, not a corner -- the retained full frame carries the recipe it was rendered from, so any edit inside the settle window (leave Develop, switch image, quit) failed the haveFull test and fell through to the proxy. The provider now DECLINES the devPreview tier there (the 256 px thumbnail tier still takes the proxy happily): flushImage drops any older entry, the loupe decodes the raw and shows the right pixels, and the provider queues that one image into the devPreview builder (buildDevPreviews, which renders full-res off the GUI thread and yields while Develop is open) so a proper preview lands shortly after. A useful check when this is suspect: the Develop diagnostics' CURRENT IMAGE section names this image's cache payload and reports its pixel size beside the sensor's, flagging one that is far smaller. DevPreviewCache::payloadPath is the lookup behind it -- the mapping only, with no recipe hash and no LRU touch, because payloads are named by index row id in hex ("537.jpg") and are otherwise impossible to identify without querying index.db by hand. (This was first prototyped as a winnow:DevelopPreviewFileName sidecar attribute; that was DROPPED because the cache and the sidecar have independent lifetimes -- an eviction or a cleared cache silently falsifies the name -- and a diagnostic that reads the index at the moment it is asked cannot go stale.) Failing that, the cache files are plain JPEGs in AppDataLocation/PreviewCache, so `sips -g pixelWidth` says the same thing.

NEITHER MAY THE EMBEDDED PREVIEW (the BUILDER's version of the same failure, fixed 2026-08-30). The provider above is only one writer; the devPreviewBuilder is the other, and it renders through MW::developPixelSource -> ImageDecoder::decodeIndependent. ImageDecoder::load() gated the full-sensor raw decode on `G::operationMode == Develop && G::useRaw`, so whenever the builder swept while the user was in PREVIEW mode the demosaic was skipped, nothing scene-linear was cached, and developPixelSource fell through to building a WorkingImage from the decoded 8-bit EMBEDDED PREVIEW -- 1024x683 for an Adobe DNG (its SubIFD1), stored as the "full resolution" preview the loupe then shows at 100%. The mode gate belongs to the BROWSE path only, exactly as in applyDevelop(): an independent decode is the focus stacker, the exporter or the preview builder asking for real pixels, not the user browsing, so the gate is now `(isIndependent || operationMode == Develop) && G::useRaw`. G::useRaw stays in the condition deliberately -- it is a persisted user choice ("Edit: Raw" / the Decode Raw button), not transient UI state, so honouring it keeps a preview consistent with what Develop shows. developPixelSource ALSO refuses a cached base that is nowhere near the CFA active area (< half the sensor long edge): WorkingImageCache is keyed by path alone and Preview mode fills it from that same embedded JPEG, so without the check a poisoned entry would keep the export -- and the preview -- rendering from a thumbnail. Symptom to recognise: a preview whose pixel width equals the file's embedded preview width (exiftool -a -G1 -s -ImageWidth lists every IFD). EXISTING BAD ENTRIES ARE NOT SELF-HEALING, since staleness is the recipe hash and that says nothing about resolution -- use Develop > Clear Developed Preview Cache to force a rebuild.

NEITHER SETTING REWRITES WHAT IS ALREADY CACHED. The staleness test is the recipe hash, not the dimensions or the encoding, so an existing preview stays valid and only NEW ones are written the new way. Lowering either setting therefore reclaims disk gradually; Develop > Clear Developed Preview Cache is the immediate route.

A devPreview AND AN EXPORT AT THE SAME QUALITY ARE THE SAME ENCODE. QImage::save(&buf, "JPG", q) and ImageExporter's QImageWriter::setQuality(q) both reach Qt's one JPEG handler, and neither path sets optimize or progressive, so equal quality means equal libjpeg tables and the same subsampling decision. What still differs is upstream: an export can render 16-bit (converted down in ImageExporter::save), can target AdobeRGB or DisplayP3 where a devPreview is always sRGB, sizes by its own rules, and can carry copied EXIF and an embedded thumbnail where a devPreview is a bare JPEG. At 8-bit sRGB, full size, no metadata copy, the compressed pixels match.

THE PIXELS ARE STILL FREE FOR THE IMAGE ON SCREEN. Full size does not mean a new render: MW::onDevelopFullResReady already produces a crisp full-resolution frame for the loupe (the settle render, ~1.3s on a 50MP raw) and now RETAINS it as developFullFrame. The provider prefers it and falls back to developFrame, the screen-resolution proxy, when the settle render has not landed. Either way a preview is a scale plus a JPEG encode off a buffer that existed anyway.

ONLY A FAITHFUL FRAME MAY BE CACHED. stackJob() applies view overrides that make the rendered frame diverge from the STORED recipe: a hovered History row (previewActive) renders a different stack entirely; the Transform preview eye off bypasses geometry, so the frame is UNCROPPED and unstraightened; the Replace eye off bypasses the heals. The full-res path adds a fourth: an interactive crop drag suppresses the crop, which makes the frame the wrong SHAPE. Caching any of those against the stored recipe's hash would key a preview to a picture it does not depict. DevelopProperties::renderMatchesStoredRecipe answers this, both render paths capture it WITH the job (the eyes can change while a render is in flight), and the provider declines when it is false. Declining clears the stale preview; the next faithful render writes a real one.

AND ONLY A CURRENT ONE. Faithful says the frame depicts the STORED recipe rather than a view override; it does not say WHICH stored recipe. A retained frame is replaced only when a render completes and is still current -- never by the edit itself -- so after an edit inside the settle window (leave Develop, switch image, quit within ~1.5s of the last change) the frame on hand is the PREVIOUS recipe's, with the same path and the same faithful flag. Writing it out is worse than writing nothing: it stamps old pixels with the new recipe's hash, and since that hash is what every staleness check downstream compares, the wrong picture then looks correct forever -- the grid thumbnail one edit behind, the loupe showing a devPreview that can be the pre-edit render. Both render paths therefore capture developRecipeKey(fPath) WITH the job (developFrameRecipe / developFullFrameRecipe) and the provider declines unless it equals the recipe being flushed. The hash is computed only for a faithful render, so an unfaithful tick pays nothing.

GEOMETRY EDITS ARM THE SETTLE RENDER TOO. Crop, warp, level and the mask tools re-render through renderDevelopPreview WITHOUT going through developParamsChange, so nothing used to re-run the full-resolution render for them: developFullFrame stayed at the recipe in force before the crop, forever. A faithful proxy render now (re)arms developFullResTimer itself, which is also why developParamsGen cannot stand in for the recipe hash above -- those callers change the recipe without bumping it.

IT WRITES BOTH TIERS, AND GATES ON BOTH. The thumbnail and the devPreview must never come from different recipes -- a grid showing one picture while the loupe shows another is worse than neither -- so if EITHER tier is missing or stale, both are rewritten. The consequence, accepted deliberately: visiting an edited image in Develop can rewrite its sidecar. Only the preview attributes and modifydate move; the recipe is passed through unchanged, so this is not the "rewriting a corrupt sidecar behind the user" case that policy elsewhere forbids.

BYPRODUCT OF RENDERING, NOT ONLY OF EDITING. flushImage runs only for DIRTY images, so an image whose recipe came from an earlier session and which the user merely VISITS never reaches it. DevelopProperties::topUpDevPreviews closes that gap: on leaving an image, leaving Develop, or quitting, if a faithful frame exists and no preview matches the recipe in force, it caches one. The frame was rendered anyway, so this still never causes a decode or a render.

RE-ENTRY IS FREE. flushAll runs before EVERY file operation (FileOps::flushPendingEdits), so a multi-image paste or ingest would call topUpDevPreviews once per file. It remembers the last (path, recipe) it brought up to date and returns immediately for a repeat, rather than re-parsing the same sidecar hundreds of times to discover there is nothing to do.

CLEARED, NOT LEFT BEHIND. When flushImage cannot obtain a preview for a new recipe it CLEARS both tiers rather than leaving the old one. A preview that no longer matches its recipe would show the user a picture they have already changed; the camera thumbnail is at least honest.

STALENESS IS ALWAYS DETECTABLE. Every preview is stamped with a 12-char hash of the develop blob it was rendered from, PLUS the two renderer facts that change the pixels without touching the recipe -- raw engine and G::autoRunDenoise (Metadata::devPreviewKey). Those two are in the key for the same reason defaultRenderKey hashes them: on the recipe alone, toggling either left every EDITED image serving pixels built under the old setting while the unedited half of the folder correctly re-rendered. Auto run matters even to a recipe that pins denoiseRaw, because unset means "follow the preference" -- hashing it unconditionally costs one extra rebuild for the images that pinned it and never shows the wrong picture. Tier 1 stores it as winnow:DevelopPreviewKey beside the pixels, written in the SAME sidecar pass as the recipe so the three can never disagree; tier 2 keeps it in the index. A reader serves a preview only when the key matches the recipe in force, so an edit made by another Winnow instance, another machine or another application can never show stale pixels -- it just misses and falls back.


⏺ Develop / Image Edit (Parametric Adjustments) > Original and Developed Previews > Read Paths

THE ICON GRID. Thumb::loadDevThumb runs FIRST in loadThumb, ahead of the embedded-JPG and every other source, and returns early -- the preview comes out of developCompositeStack already EXIF-rotated and cropped, so it must not go through the scale-and-checkOrientation tail that follows. It declines outright when the user has asked for Original.

THE IMAGE CACHE. Outside Develop mode, ImageDecoder::loadDevPreview serves the devPreview INSTEAD OF DECODING THE FILE (Cache/imagedecoder.cpp), before the file is even opened. This is what makes browsing a folder of developed raws as fast as browsing JPEGs: no demosaic, no develop pipeline, no float buffer, just a JPEG decode. Three consequences the decoder has to honour:

- decode() must skip rotate() AND applyDevelop(): the pixels arrive oriented, cropped and developed. loadedFromDevPreview says so.
- colorManage() must treat the source as sRGB, not as the image file's embedded profile. The devPreview left the pipeline through OutputTransform in sRGB, so using the raw file's profile would be a WRONG transform, not merely a redundant one. An empty buffer makes ICC::transform assume sRGB, which is exactly right.
- The recipe hash has to be reachable from a decoder thread, which cannot call DevelopProperties on the GUI thread. G::DevPreviewKeyColumn carries it, filled by Metadata::parseSidecar in the same pass that sets the develop badge -- no extra I/O -- and refreshed by MW::devPreviewUpdated when an edit is flushed. That same function drops the path from the image cache, because the QImage cached there was decoded from the old recipe.
- An EMPTY key column no longer means "no preview". It means the image has no recipe, and for a raw with a sensor decoder there may still be a cached DEFAULT RENDER, so loadDevPreview SYNTHESIZES Metadata::defaultRenderKey and looks that up instead. It has to be synthesized rather than read because the key column is filled from the sidecar and an unedited image has no sidecar to fill it from. Any other format still returns false: its file already is the default render.

IN DEVELOP MODE the loupe must show the live render, so the devPreview is used only as the interim placeholder during the sensor decode: MW::devPreview feeds ImageView::loadImageInterim's `substitute` argument, replacing the camera JPG while the raw decodes, and the rendering-hint chip says "Cached preview - decoding raw" so the later swap to the real render does not read as an unexplained flicker.

ENTERING DEVELOP IS A SEPARATE HOOK from selecting an image. MW::fileSelectionChange only reaches its placeholder branch on an image-cache MISS, but a Preview -> Develop switch is a cache HIT, so it never goes near that branch and the loupe would sit on the untouched camera render for the whole re-decode. MW::setOperationMode therefore paints the cached preview itself, through the SAME loadImageInterim path. Not setDevelopPreview: that one is for the drag case, where only the pixels change and the displayed dimensions stay the current image's -- which is precisely wrong on a mode switch, because a CROPPED preview has a different shape from the uncropped image still on screen, and presenting it at that image's display size stretches it to fill the frame until the render lands. loadImageInterim fits from the pixmap's own bounding rect, so the crop's aspect ratio survives, and it captures and restores zoom/pan across the swap.


⏺ Develop / Image Edit (Parametric Adjustments) > Original and Developed Previews > The Index, Orphans and the Sweep

WHY TIER 2 FILES ARE NAMED BY AN OPAQUE ID. Lookup is by absolute path, but the file on disk is named by a counter, never by a hash of the path. That is what makes file-operation sync cheap: a rename or a move rewrites one row's path column -- no file to rename, nothing to re-encode, O(1) per image. Deleting an image unlinks one file.

THE INDEX IS SQLITE, SIZED FOR 250,000 IMAGES. It began as a JSON array held entirely in memory and rewritten in full on every put, which is genuinely cheaper than a dependency at a few hundred entries and indefensible at a quarter of a million: the array is tens of MB, the in-memory copy is ~50 MB of paths, and a background preview build over a large library rewrote the whole file once per image. It is now the devpreview table in AppDataLocation/PreviewCache/index.db -- an indexed lookup instead of a hash of every path, an O(1) write instead of a full rewrite, real transactions, and a file format that survives being extended.

THAT LAST POINT IS THE REASON IT IS CALLED CacheDb AND NOT "the preview database" (Cache/cachedb.h). It is Winnow's local index database: one file for every derived, rebuildable index the app keeps about images it has seen, with a versioned, ADDITIVE schema (PRAGMA user_version) and one connection per thread. A connection is released when its thread ends -- but NOT when the application is already gone, which for thread-pool threads is when they usually end: Qt's SQL registry is being torn down by then, so touching it warns once per thread on every quit and achieves nothing, and the connection dies with the process regardless. Indexed keywords are the expected next tenant, and they belong in the same file so that a keyword search and a preview lookup share a connection, a transaction boundary and one thing to back up, clear or repair. Nothing irreplaceable goes in it -- everything is derived from the images and their sidecars, which is what lets the failure policy be "move it aside and rebuild".

WAL, AND synchronous=NORMAL. A preview read on a decoder thread never blocks the write on the GUI thread. Full fsync-per-commit durability would buy nothing here -- the worst a lost commit costs is a re-render -- at a price paid on every develop flush. MW::closeEvent and the startup sweep still call save(), which now folds the write-ahead log back into the database rather than writing an index.

EXISTING CACHES ARE CARRIED OVER, not abandoned. The first open of a database with no rows imports any index.json beside it and renames it index.json.migrated -- renamed rather than deleted, so a bad import is recoverable by hand. Without that step every upgrading user's cache would read as empty and reconcile() would delete every payload in it. tst_devpreview::legacyJsonIndexIsImported pins it.

THE RECIPE HASH IDENTIFIES A RECIPE, NOT AN IMAGE. This is the subtle one. Presets, Paste Settings and multi-image edits give whole folders a BYTE-IDENTICAL recipe, so the hash cannot tell two images apart at all. If a path is reused by a different image behind Winnow's back -- renamed in Finder while Winnow was closed, restored from a backup, renumbered by the camera -- the path matches, the hash matches, and the previous occupant's picture is served as though it were correct, with every staleness check downstream agreeing. Each row therefore also records the SOURCE IMAGE's length and modification time, and a hit needs those to agree too.

Nothing on disk at all is deliberately NOT treated as a mismatch: a missing source is the sweep's demote case (the file is in the trash, or its volume is ejected) and the preview is still the right picture of it. Only a file that is present and DIFFERENT drops the entry. Rows imported from the JSON index carry no stamp; they are grandfathered rather than discarded -- re-rendering a quarter of a million previews to close a rare window is the worse trade -- and the first sweep, which already stats every source image, stamps them. tst_devpreview::reusedPathDoesNotServeThePreviousImage pins it.

ORPHANS (tier 2 only). Previews are disposable, so the design makes orphans harmless rather than trying to prevent them. Three layers: (1) an LRU byte cap, which is the load-bearing bound -- the dominant growth case is thousands of images that all still exist, which no sweep would ever reclaim; (2) a one-shot sweep at the first folder load, run off the GUI thread, which DEMOTES entries whose source file is missing rather than deleting them, so a file restored from the trash finds its preview intact; (3) the FileOps hooks, for operations Winnow performs itself.

THE SWEEP MUST NEVER TOUCH AN UNMOUNTED VOLUME. Winnow browses memory cards and external drives constantly. Each entry records the mount point it was created under, and the sweep skips any entry whose volume is not currently mounted -- otherwise ejecting a drive and restarting would demote, and then evict, every preview on it. This is the single most important behaviour in the cache and is pinned by tst_devpreview::sweepSkipsUnmountedVolume.

NOT AT SHUTDOWN. The sweep runs at the first folder load, never in closeEvent: a force-quit or a crash skips shutdown work entirely, and closeEvent is already doing a synchronous flushAll plus thread teardown.

EVERY MUTATION IS COMMITTED WHEN IT HAPPENS, for the same reason. The index used to be written only by closeEvent, so an abnormal exit did not merely forget the previews written that session -- reconcile() deletes files the index does not name, so the next launch DELETED them, while the tier-1 thumbnails written to the sidecars in the same flush survived. The result was the one state this whole design exists to prevent: the grid showing the developed picture and the loupe showing the camera's, with no repair short of re-rendering the folder. tst_devpreview::putCommitsIndexWithoutAnExplicitSave pins it. With SQLite this is simply what a commit is, rather than a full-file rewrite the put has to pay for.

MOVES AND DELETES COUNT AS MUTATIONS. onMoved used to change only the in-memory index, so a crash after a rename lost it -- and what survived was worse than a forgotten preview: an index still naming the SOURCE path. Let something else take that path later and the row describes a different image, and if the two share a recipe (which a preset makes the normal case) the hash agrees. The source stamp above is the backstop for a rename Winnow never saw; committing the move is the fix for the ones it did.

LRU HAS ONE-SECOND RESOLUTION. Entries last used in the same second are tied, and evictLocked falls back to insertion order (id) between them. Harmless in use -- a tie means they really were used together -- but it is why tst_devpreview::lruEvictsLeastRecentlyUsedAtFullSize has to let the clock move before asserting an order.

THE INDEX IS OPENED LAZILY, ON FIRST USE, and that is the ONLY load path -- nothing calls load() or setCacheDir() in the app, and setCacheDir deliberately does not open anything. An explicit init call is a thing a caller can forget, and forgetting it fails silently and destructively: the cache reads as empty every session so every prior-session preview misses, and nextId restarts at 1 so the first new preview OVERWRITES the cache file of an unrelated image. nextId now comes from MAX(id) in the table rather than a stored counter, so it cannot restart at 1 even if the counter were lost. That is exactly what happened when the load was tied to an init call MW did not make; tst_devpreview::lazyLoadPreservesIdsAcrossSessions pins it. setMaxBytes is safe to call before any load, which is why MW::loadSettings applies the preference there.

A CORRUPT INDEX IS NOT RECOVERABLE BY SCANNING, and does not need to be. The id in a filename says nothing about which image it belonged to, so an unreferenced <id>.jpg is dead weight; reconcile() deletes those and drops rows whose file is gone. A database that will not open or will not migrate is MOVED ASIDE (index.db.corrupt.<timestamp>, with its WAL sidecars) and recreated, because the alternative is a user who can never cache anything again and no way to tell them why. If SQLite is unavailable altogether, every get simply misses and the app runs without a preview cache rather than refusing to start.

RECONCILE IS ONE DIRECTORY LISTING AND ONE TABLE SCAN, matched through a set of ids. The obvious shape -- stat the payload named by each row, then list the folder to find strays -- is two passes over the folder and a stat per row; at 250,000 entries that is a quarter of a million stat calls at launch. Reading the names alone is one pass.

LOOKUP IS BY A NORMALISED KEY, NOT THE RAW PATH. The same file reaches Winnow spelled several ways, and a byte-exact index treats each spelling as a different image: DataModel stores QFileInfo::filePath() (the path AS CONSTRUCTED -- not cleaned, not absolutised), drop and paste build destinations by concatenation (dropDir + "/" + name, which doubles the separator when the folder already ends in one), Finder and Explorer drags arrive through QUrl::toLocalFile(), Windows mixes '\' and '/' and varies the drive letter's case, and the same folder can be reached through a symlink, through a share mounted under another name, or as /Volumes/X and /System/Volumes/Data/X. A mismatch never showed the wrong picture, but it cost real things: a put under a second spelling INSERTED A SECOND ROW, so the same image was cached twice at several MB each and the byte cap bit sooner; a get under a second spelling silently re-decoded the raw; and the FileOps move and delete hooks quietly found nothing to update. Every row now carries both -- path as the filesystem spelled it (what gets stat'd, and what the diagnostics report shows) and pathkey for every WHERE clause. Cache/pathkey.h is shared, because the key has to be computed identically by the code that stores a row, the code that looks one up, and the schema migration that backfills the column -- and because the keyword tables to come will key on the same thing. tst_devpreview::pathSpellingDoesNotSplitTheEntry pins it.

CASE IS FOLDED IN THE KEY, following the decision the codebase had already made (DevPreviewCache::isCachePath and DataModel both compare paths case-insensitively, because both supported platforms are case-insensitive by default). The edge case that leaves is a case-SENSITIVE volume -- APFS can be formatted that way, and Linux-hosted network shares usually are -- holding two files whose names differ only in case. They fold to one key. That cannot show the wrong picture: the source stamp disagrees and the entry is dropped, so the two images take turns MISSING rather than lying. Wasteful in a situation close to hypothetical; a wrong photograph would not be acceptable at any frequency. Unicode is normalised to NFC on macOS, where the filesystem returns decomposed forms and QUrl does not, so an accented name from a folder scan and the same name from a Finder drag are otherwise different strings for one file.

NO PAYLOAD I/O HAPPENS UNDER THE CACHE MUTEX. A full-resolution entry is several MB. get() used to hold the one cache mutex across the read, which serialised all of ImageCache's decoderCount threads on one disk read at a time -- undoing the parallel read-ahead that makes browsing developed raws fast -- and put() held it across a multi-MB QSaveFile write plus commit, stalling every decoder thread once per image for the length of a background preview build. Both now take the lock, decide, drop it, do the I/O, and take it again to record the result. Note the serialisation was entirely self-imposed: WAL was chosen so a read on a decoder thread would not block the write on the GUI thread, and the mutex was re-imposing exactly what it avoids.

RELEASING THE LOCK OPENS ONE WINDOW, and both of its outcomes are safe. Between the lookup and the read, the row can be evicted or replaced by a put. An evicted payload fails to open and answers with a miss, which is what a miss looks like anyway. A replaced one is caught by re-checking the id before doing anything destructive -- get() only drops a row whose payload is missing if it is still the row it looked at, so a put that landed meanwhile is not undone. put() has the mirror of this: it re-reads the row after writing, and removes the earlier payload if the id moved underneath it.

THE LONG PASSES DO NOT HOLD THE LOCK. sweep() stats one source image per row, which on a network volume is slow; holding the cache mutex throughout would block every get() for the duration, and that is a frozen loupe. It works in pages of 512 rows, taking the lock to read a page and again to apply the verdicts, and stats in between. Rows are taken in id order so the cursor survives deletions, and a row inserted or removed mid-sweep is simply seen or not seen -- both correct, and the pass is idempotent. The mount table is also walked ONCE per sweep rather than once per row (a statfs per volume, previously a quarter of a million walks).

CLEARING. Develop > Clear Developed Preview Cache empties the on-disk tier after a confirmation that reports how much is being deleted. It does NOT touch the 256px thumbnails: those live in the sidecars and are the image's own property, so deleting them would be an edit to the user's files rather than a cache operation.


⏺ Develop / Image Edit (Parametric Adjustments) > Original and Developed Previews > The Preview Cache Folder Is Read-Only

THE FOLDER IS BROWSABLE, AND THAT IS THE PROBLEM. The tier-2 cache lives in AppDataLocation/PreviewCache and holds nothing but JPEGs, so the folder panel will happily open it: the user gets a grid of their developed images with no hint that these are not their photographs. Everything Winnow does to an image folder is destructive here. A cache file is named by an opaque id that ONLY the index can attribute to an image, so renaming or moving one detaches a preview from its image permanently. reconcile() DELETES any file the index does not name, so a file copied in -- and any sidecar Winnow writes beside a preview when it is rated, labelled, rotated or developed -- is silently deleted at the next launch. And a preview trashed by hand leaves an index entry pointing at nothing.

SO IT IS READ-ONLY, ANNOUNCED UP FRONT. Selecting the folder shows a popup saying the images here can be viewed but not edited, renamed, rated, deleted or developed, and drops out of Develop mode if it was active. Everything that writes is then GREYED with that reason while the folder is loaded (MW::isPreviewCacheFolderLoaded, applied in enableSelectionDependentMenus): rename, delete, paste, the ratings and labels menus, rotate, and Develop mode itself -- a disabled QAction greys the menu item AND makes its shortcut inert, so the reason reaches the user through the Disabled Shortcut Feedback path rather than as a popup after the fact.

THE UI GATE IS NOT THE PROTECTION. It is the explanation. The protection is three refusals below it, so no future caller -- a drag-and-drop onto the folder, a script, a context menu that forgets to ask -- can get through: FileOps::copyFile / moveFile / trashFile refuse on either side of the path, the three sidecar writers (Metadata::writeOrientation / writeDevelopSidecar / writeXMP) refuse to write into it, and MW::setOperationMode refuses to ENTER Develop there. MW::deleteFolder and MW::pasteFiles refuse at the folder level, which is one step above what FileOps can see. Each refusal logs a G::issue warning and changes nothing on disk. The one predicate they all share is DevPreviewCache::isCachePath (case-insensitive, matches the folder itself and anything under it); tst_devpreview::isCachePathIdentifiesTheProtectedFolder and tst_fileops::refusesToWriteInThePreviewCacheFolder pin it.

READING IS UNAFFECTED, deliberately. Browsing the cache is how a user answers "what is in here, and is it worth the disk?" -- the diagnostics report the same thing in numbers. Emptying it is a supported operation, but through Develop > Clear Developed Preview Cache, which drops the index with the files; deleting the JPEGs by hand would leave the index describing a cache that is not there.


⏺ Develop / Image Edit (Parametric Adjustments) > Building devPreviews (Edited Images and Unedited Raws)

THE GAP. A devPreview is a byproduct of a render that happened anyway, which covers the image on screen and nothing else. Every image edited in an earlier session, and every target of a multi-image paste (which has no proxy in memory and correctly CLEARS its preview), has a recipe but no render -- so no preview, so the grid shows the camera thumbnail and the loupe has to decode the raw.

Closing that gap means DECODING AND RENDERING an image purely to cache it, which is exactly what the byproduct rule exists to avoid. So it never happens unasked. Either the user runs Develop > Build Developed Previews, or they turn on "Build developed previews in background" (G::buildDevPreviewsInBackground, off by default) and accept the cost. Main/devpreviewbuilder.cpp.

TWO POPULATIONS ARE BUILT, and the second is much the larger. EDITED images are keyed on the hash of their recipe (Metadata::devPreviewKey) -- the gap described above, usually a handful of images per folder. UNEDITED RAWS -- any raw Winnow has a sensor decoder for (RawFormat::HasSensorDecoder) -- are keyed on the RENDERER instead (Metadata::defaultRenderKey), and what gets cached is the DEFAULT RENDER: the pipeline run with identity adjustments, which is what the user would see on entering Develop without touching a slider. MW::devPreviewBuildKey decides which of the two an image gets, or empty for a file that should have no preview at all (a JPEG, or a raw with no sensor decoder -- for those the file on disk already IS the default render, so a preview would be a re-encode of it: pure cost, no gain).

THE SECOND POPULATION EXISTS PURELY FOR BROWSING SPEED. With it cached, ImageDecoder::loadDevPreview serves a raw from a JPEG decode instead of a demosaic, so an unedited raw folder browses like a folder of JPEGs -- and shows WINNOW's rendering of the sensor data rather than the camera's embedded JPEG. The cost is paid once, up front, and it is steep: a thousand-raw folder is a thousand full decodes and composites, run one at a time, so hours rather than seconds, and several GB of cache.
